# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-09

## Embodied AI
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Computer-Use Agents Are Embodied Generalists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09847v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In many areas of AI, general-purpose models have increasingly replaced handcrafted methods and specialized architectures. We argue that embodied AI is poised for a similar shift. Our key claim is that embodied tasks can be formulated as interaction with digital software. Specifically, we build software that renders an agent's view of a physical environment on a screen and provides controls for acting in it. A computer-use agent (CUA) can then perform embodied tasks through the same screenshots and keyboard and mouse actions that it uses to operate ordinary software. Using a single CUA design and changing only the software, our entries win first-, second-, third-, and sixth-place prizes across four ECCV workshop competitions, without any competition-specific agent tuning. These results suggest that the skills CUAs learn on software, such as visual grounding, precise clicking, and exploration, already carry over to embodied tasks, giving CUAs a far broader scope in embodied AI than previously realized. We therefore advocate that computer-use agents can be embodied generalists. Further, we advocate that, by following the training strategy that made computer-use agents general, embodied AI can converge on a single general model as well, ushering in a new era of general embodied AI. Website: https://pranjal2041.github.io/cua-embodied</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 3 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[Vision to Geometry: 3D Spatial Memory for Sequential Embodied MLLM Reasoning and Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02458v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied agents are expected to assist humans by actively exploring unknown environments and reasoning about spatial contexts. When deployed in real life, agents often face sequential tasks where each new task follows the completion of the previous one and may include infeasible objectives, such as searching for non-existent objects. However, most existing research focuses on isolated goals, overlooking the core challenge of sequential tasks: the ability to reuse spatial knowledge accumulated from previous explorations to guide subsequent reasoning and exploration. In this work, we investigate this underexplored yet practically significant embodied AI challenge. Specifically, we propose 3DSPMR, a 3D SPatial Memory Reasoning framework that utilizes Field-of-View (FoV) coverage as an explicit geometric prior. By integrating FoV-based constraints, 3DSPMR significantly enhances an agent's memory, reasoning, and exploration capabilities across sequential tasks. To facilitate research in this area, we further introduce SEER-Bench, a novel Sequential Embodied Exploration and Reasoning Benchmark that spans two foundational tasks: Embodied Question Answering (EQA) and Embodied Multi-modal Navigation (EMN). SEER-Bench uniquely incorporates both feasible and infeasible tasks to provide a rigorous and comprehensive evaluation of agent performance. Extensive experiments verify that 3DSPMR achieves substantial performance gains on both sequential EQA and EMN tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Open-HumanWAM: An Open Recipe for Scaling Human and Robot Experience for Embodied Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06817v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric human video offers a scalable source of manipulation priors for robot learning, yet translating raw data scale into generalizable robot policies demands rigorous data curation and training recipes. We introduce Open-HumanWAM, the first fully open recipe for scaling egocentric pretraining to 50,000 hours, anchored by Open-HumanWAM-50K---a curated egocentric video corpus densely annotated with hand motion and natural language. Leveraging this corpus, we systematically investigate pure human pretraining via closed-loop simulation evaluations and uncover an empirical log-linear scaling law linking pretraining data hours to downstream robot success rates. Grounded in these findings, we develop HumanWAM-Vanilla, a world--action model pretrained exclusively on 50k hours of egocentric human video. Upon robot post-training, HumanWAM-Vanilla reaches 92.08% success on RoboTwin 2.0 Clean2Clean and 68.88% on Clean2Random, alongside a 14.17% mean success rate and a 19.00 mean progress score on RoboDojo. To push the capability frontier further, we explore joint human--robot pretraining with HumanWAM-Goal, a visual-goal-conditioned world--action model. Hybrid data scaling unlocks broad zero-shot generalization across both simulation and physical benchmarks, propelling post-trained RoboTwin 2.0 performance to new state-of-the-art levels: 92.70% on Clean2Clean and a substantial leap to 84.60% on Clean2Random. On physical robots, HumanWAM-Goal further exhibits emergent embodied in-context learning, dynamically adapting its manipulation behavior to unseen visual goals without parameter updates. To support reproducible scaling research in embodied AI, we open-source the Open-HumanWAM-50K corpus, data curation and pretraining pipelines, alongside the pretrained and post-trained HumanWAM checkpoints, inference code, and simulation evaluation suites.</p></details> | <details><summary>GitHu...</summary><p>GitHub: https://github.com/DAGroup-PKU/HumanNet/tree/Open-HumanWAM Project website: https://dagroup-pku.github.io/open-humanwam/</p></details> |
| **[NeoWorld-Pro: Programming Interactive Scenes from Monocular Images for Embodied Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24212v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The advancement of Embodied AI necessitates high-quality simulation assets that faithfully mirror the real world. However, transforming raw visual observations into simulation-ready scenes remains challenging due to the lack of physical grounding and scene-level interactivity in current image-to-URDF methods. We propose NeoWorld-Pro, a framework that reformulates monocular scene reconstruction as procedural programming for interactive 3D environments. Leveraging the zero-shot reasoning and code synthesis capabilities of MLLMs, NeoWorld-Pro converts a single RGB image into executable programs specifying object geometry, articulation, and physical properties. A physics-in-the-loop mechanism then iteratively refines the generated programs by validating their execution in a physics engine, enforcing physically plausible articulations, valid object compositions and interactions, and accurate spatial relationships. Experiments show that NeoWorld-Pro outperforms open-loop and prior monocular reconstruction methods, while enabling complex downstream tasks such as stable stacking and fine-grained manipulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://neoworldproject.github.io/neoworld-pro-website/</p></details> |
| **[EgoKit: Towards Unified Low-Cost Egocentric Data Collection with Heterogeneous Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16797v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric video is increasingly used as a data source for robot learning, activity understanding, and embodied AI research, but collecting it at scale remains fragmented in practice: each candidate host device, such as an Android phone, iPhone, iPad, smart glasses, or extended reality (XR) headset, exposes a different SDK, a different policy on raw camera access, and different limitations on external USB cameras and on-device tracking. Synchronized ego-view and wrist-view capture is therefore typically obtained by either committing to a single proprietary platform or building one-off rigs that do not transfer across devices. To address this gap, we present EgoKit, a toolkit that exposes the same egocentric recording workflow across six heterogeneous host devices. Across all supported devices, EgoKit presents the same recording interaction and produces locally stored video with a uniform log format; on XR headsets, it additionally logs head pose and OpenXR-standard 26-joint hand tracking aligned to the video streams. The companion accessories, including two wrist cameras with mounts, a head strap, and a USB-C hub, add wrist-view capture to any supported host without custom hardware fabrication. To validate that EgoKit data can train robot policies, we replicated HumanEgo with 41 demonstrations recorded by EgoKit-PICO: in simulation, policies trained on 20 or 40 of them placed a cube on a tray in 60-74% of 100 unseen layouts, about as often as policies trained on Project Aria recordings of the same layouts. EgoKit is available at https://egokit.chuange.org/.</p></details> |  |
| **[PanoWorld: Geometry-Consistent Panoramic Video World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15391v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present PanoWorld, a panoramic video world model that generates geometry-consistent 360$\degree$ video from a single image and a caption. Existing panoramic video methods optimize primarily for visual realism and do not explicitly constrain the underlying 3D scene state, producing outputs that appear plausible yet exhibit inconsistent depth, broken correspondences, and implausible motion across the spherical surface. We address this gap by framing panoramic video generation as a geometry- and dynamics-consistent latent state modeling problem rather than pure visual synthesis. Building on a pre-trained perspective video world model, we introduce two lightweight regularizers: a depth consistency loss against pseudo ground-truth panoramic depth, and a trajectory consistency loss that supervises the 3D world-frame positions of tracked points across time. We further apply spherical-geometry-aware adaptation to the conditioning and positional encoding. We additionally introduce PanoGeo, a unified geometry-aware panoramic video dataset with consistent depth, trajectory, and prompt annotations across diverse real and synthetic sources, used for both training and stratified evaluation. Experiments show that PanoWorld improves geometric consistency over prior panoramic generation methods while maintaining competitive visual realism, establishing that panoramic video generation must be treated as a geometric modeling problem to support the holistic spatial understanding requirements of embodied AI applications. Code is available at https://github.com/ostadabbas/PanoWorld.</p></details> |  |
| **[OdorSpaces: Visual-Olfactory Embodied Navigation in 3D Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05738v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Olfaction provides critical information about airborne chemical cues, yet it remains largely underexplored in embodied AI, with limited prior machine olfaction work focusing primarily on semantic odor perception. We introduce OdorSpaces, a physics-based simulation platform and benchmark for visual-olfactory embodied navigation. OdorSpaces augments Habitat-Sim with 3D odor transport and dispersion simulated by Fire Dynamics Simulator (FDS), enabling agents to navigate photorealistic indoor environments using both visual and olfactory observations. The benchmark includes two representative source-seeking scenarios: invisible gas-leak localization and early-stage fire localization. We develop an initial multimodal reinforcement learning policy that integrates visual observations and olfactory measurements over time. Experiments show that the policy outperforms a diverse set of baselines, establishing a strong reference for future research on visual-olfactory embodied navigation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 10th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL 2026). 20 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[Embodied AI for Scientific Discovery: From Physical Action to Scientific Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04371v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific experiments are increasingly selected, executed and interpreted by learned systems that act through physical interfaces. Robot learning builds physical competence, whereas AI for science chooses which experiment to run, each reporting different endpoints. This review treats their interface as one problem, embodied AI for science, where the experiment itself is partially observed: specimen state, apparatus state and measurement processes are latent, actions are costly and irreversible, and missingness depends on execution and outcome. We organize the literature by three closures operating at distinct levels: operational closure (the experiment realized is the one intended), epistemic closure (the result updates beliefs or selects subsequent experiments) and evidential closure (the record supports a claim). We analyze workflows through a record-linked evidence cycle - Record, Diagnose, Calibrate, Confirm - as analytic functions with feedback. A taxonomy crosses these closures with six delegated decisions and five embodiment classes. Our formulation separates commanded, realized and observed conditions and models diagnosis with explicit resource, risk and sample-change costs. We compare experiment-selection methods by terminal decision, review perception, protocol compilation, visuomotor policies, world models, recovery and transfer by evidence emission requirements, and compare chemistry, materials, biology, microscopy and field workflows by what their records establish. An appendix provides an evidence map of 28 workflows (26 physical, two software-boundary), characterizing validation pathways and claim confirmation requirements. We propose evaluation that separates actions, valid experiments, campaigns and confirmed claims, framing open challenges as controlled comparisons with measurable failure conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>46 pa...</summary><p>46 pages, 10 figures, 14 tables. Review article</p></details> |
| **[RoboScope Kit: Unified Interfaces for Fast and Scalable Embodied-AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03850v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulation benchmarks are the standard way to compare robot policies, but each benchmark brings its own evaluator, observation layout, and action convention, and evaluation is slow: 500 RoboTwin Easy episodes with one simulator and one model on one GPU take 359.7 minutes. Profiling the official StarVLA evaluator shows that simulation occupies CPU cores while the GPU averages 14.3% utilization. RoboScope Kit wraps each model once in a Python class called a Policy, and each benchmark once in an adapter around the benchmark's own evaluator, so the benchmark's rules and scoring stay unchanged. Scheduling runs simulator workers and Policy replicas as separate pools, sizes both from measured probes, and shards work deterministically across GPU instances; runtime optimization renders only the observations an action-chunk Policy reads. Adapters cover six benchmark families. On LIBERO-Long with a StarVLA checkpoint on one 48 GiB RTX 4090, the concurrency search selects twelve simulator workers sharing three Policy replicas, which evaluate 500 episodes 4.45$\times$ faster than the official StarVLA evaluation with equivalent success rates, 2.78$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 1.52$\times$ faster than vla-eval at its tuned configuration. With CUDA MPS, which lets the replicas run their kernels concurrently, the search selects sixteen workers sharing four replicas, 6.16$\times$ faster than StarVLA, 3.85$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 2.10$\times$ faster than vla-eval. On RoboTwin Easy, four GPUs with chunk-boundary observation complete the 500 episodes in 14.4 minutes, 25.04$\times$ faster than the single-GPU baseline, with identical success outcomes and terminal steps.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 9 figures. Project page: https://roboscope.pages.dev/ Code: https://github.com/MINT-SJTU/RoboScope-Kit</p></details> |
| **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.</p></details> | <details><summary>Updat...</summary><p>Update project address</p></details> |
| **[When the Past Shapes Action: Understanding Embodied Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41688v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in generative foundation models have expanded the capabilities of embodied AI, enabling increasingly general navigation, manipulation, and long-horizon task execution. However, effective interaction with dynamic environments requires more than only strong perception and reasoning: agents must act with partial knowledge and retain, retrieve, and update information from past observations, actions, and experiences. This survey examines embodied memory as a mechanism for preserving decision-relevant information beyond the current observation. We organize existing approaches along two complementary dimensions: six fundamental memory requirements, spanning world-state persistence, task progress, belief maintenance, and experiential learning; and eight architectural categories, including recurrent states, fast weights, visual and symbolic representations, spatial memory, and experience-based systems. We analyze their capabilities and limitations, and examine how existing benchmarks evaluate memory-dependent behavior, distinguishing genuine use of historical information from general task performance. Finally, we identify open challenges in selecting relevant experiences, maintaining consistent beliefs in changing environments, and consolidating knowledge across episodes. Our analysis provides a framework for understanding memory as an essential component of reliable, adaptive, and lifelong-horizon embodied intelligence.</p></details> |  |
| **[Gym-Anything for Robotics: Scaling physical simulations with digital agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40565v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic, fast robotics simulations could enable large-scale training of embodied AI models that transfer to the real world. However, despite decades of progress, available simulations cover only a narrow range of real-world tasks and physical phenomena. The core bottleneck is that creating better simulations requires significant expert effort and therefore does not scale. To address this, we introduce Gym-Anything for Robotics, a framework in which coding agents create realistic robotics simulations. Our core insight is that simulation creation is a coding agent task, so improvements in coding agents translate directly into richer and more faithful simulations. We select simulations based on U.S. GDP data to cover economically valuable physical work, define what makes a good simulation and a good task, and develop an adversarial creation-audit loop that checks each simulation and task against these definitions. Using this pipeline, we build RoboWorld, a set of 25 simulations with 25 test and 175 training RL environments of varying difficulty. Even the best frontier models fail on every RoboWorld test task, achieving an average reward of only 0.06. Building on RoboWorld, we introduce several new ways for frontier models to improve, including in-context learning across repeated attempts, a privileged teacher that reads the simulator state and teaches a student in context, self-assisted learning from the model's own privileged experience, and code policies that generate demonstrations across varied scenes to train vision-language-action models (VLAs) such as $π_{0.5}$. Each of these substantially improves performance. We release our pipeline, RL environments, and benchmark to facilitate future research. Code and data are available at https://gym-anything.github.io/robotics</p></details> |  |
| **[UniffordInteractor: Reasoning the Unified Affordance for Human and Multi-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39745v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deriving plausible and dynamic Human Object Interaction (HOI) has gained tremendous progress in Embodied AI and Immersive AR/VR. While existing HOI methods have effectively explored human interaction with individual or fixed objects, they still show limitations in complex multi-object interaction scenes. Meanwhile, previous approaches focus more on the contact information held by human-object relation, overlooking the significance of the contact situation of various entities (i.e., object-object). To address these limitations, we propose UniffordInteractor, a Reasoning-then-Interacting framework that leverages unified affordance for realistic and long-horizon human and multi-object interaction modeling. At the reasoning stage, we devise a Unified Affordance Reasoner (UAR) that employs a fine-tuned 3D Multimodal Large Language Model for unified object-centric affordance prediction. At the interacting stage, we propose Hierarchical Interaction Generator (HIG), which formulates the interaction generation as a hierarchical (e.g., root to body to hand to objects) autoregressive prediction, conditioned on the obtained unified affordance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that UniffordInteractor surpasses SOTA counterparts in human and multi-object interaction generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 11 figures. Published in ACM Transactions on Graphics (Proceedings of SIGGRAPH Asia 2026)</p></details> |
| **[OmniDex: Scaling Dexterous Hand Grasping to Diverse Cluttered Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11194v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dexterous grasping is the foundational primitive in embodied AI, demanding massive data to train robust models. As real-world data collection is expensive, simulation has become the mainstream paradigm. Yet, while cluttered scenes best reflect real-world applications, learning to grasp within them is bottlenecked by a critical scarcity of large-scale data. To resolve this, we curate high-quality 3D objects and supporting bases, proposing a scalable seed-and-filter strategy that bypasses sluggish scene-level optimization. This yields an unprecedented benchmark comprising over 2.6 million scenes and 0.4B scene-specific grasp ground truths, featuring diverse realistic layouts paired with rich semantic and geometric observations. Furthermore, we introduce the OmniDex model to overcome the grasp multimodality and last-millimeter precision errors plaguing current generative models. By coupling Soft Winner-Takes-All learning with human-inspired physical constraints during training, and utilizing physics-driven ranking, our approach achieves robust dexterous grasping without the latency of post-optimization. Experimental results show that OmniDex model achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization across diverse scenes, views, and unseen objects.</p></details> |  |
| **[InHabit: Scaling 3D Human-Scene Interaction Data With Image Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19673v3)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and human-centric 3D scene understanding require data of people meaningfully interacting with diverse environments, yet such data remains scarce. Real-world capture has steadily pushed toward more scenes, but is bound to studios and at most around a hundred scenes, while synthetic datasets scale easily but place people without regard to what the scene affords. We argue that useful human-scene interaction data requires diversity in both scenes and interactions, and we show this with our proposed data engine InHabit. InHabit is a fully automatic engine that renders an existing 3D scene, uses 2D foundation models to propose and depict context-appropriate interactions, and lifts them into SMPL-X bodies, where the known scene geometry makes lifting well-constrained. Applied to Habitat-Matterport3D, InHabit produces InHabitants, which pairs photorealistic images with SMPL-X bodies and complete scene geometry across ~6.6k rooms, about 60x more scenes than the largest captured dataset. We illustrate the quality and value of such large-scale human-scene interaction data through a variety of experiments. In a perceptual study, our interactions are preferred over two prior placement methods in 78% of cases. Across three tasks - 1) contact estimation, 2) human-scene reconstruction, and 3) generating humans in scenes - training with InHabitants outperforms training on synthetic data with exact ground truth (BEDLAM), motion-capture data (TRUMANS), and real RGB-D captures (PROX); e.g., GRAFT trained on InHabitants reaches a contact F1 of 0.594 on PROX, versus 0.492 when trained on PROX itself. Project page: https://virtualhumans.mpi-inf.mpg.de/inhabit/</p></details> |  |
| **[POD-RoPE: Rotary Position Encoding over Physical Observation Distributions for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33745v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied perception requires positional representations that capture both physical geometry and the spatial and temporal extent of observations. We introduce POD-RoPE (Physical Observation Distribution RoPE), which represents token observation footprints as distributions over camera centers, viewing directions, and sampling times. Under independent sampling, expected rotations encode relative relationships between distributions; when each distribution collapses to a single address, the construction recovers pointwise RoPE. The same rotation statistics support Q/K matching and coherence-dependent V/O transformations, allowing observation geometry to guide both information selection and content aggregation. Per-token pre-aggregation avoids increasing sequence length or processing pairs of observation nodes. We evaluate POD-RoPE across four robot policy architectures, simulation and real-world manipulation, and complementary reconstruction, depth-estimation, and novel-view-synthesis tasks. Integrating POD-RoPE into Fast-WAM improves mean success from 91.83% to 92.88% across all 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks, and from 32.5% to 52.5% on two real-robot tasks on Sharpa North. Controlled reconstruction ablations support the contributions of distribution encoding and V/O transformations. In H20 model-block benchmarks, POD-RoPE achieves median forward-plus-backward speedups of $1.91\times$ over P-RoPE and $5.34\times$ over RayRoPE, with a median latency overhead of 17.4% over Standard RoPE. These results support physical observation distributions as an effective and computationally efficient basis for geometry-aware attention.</p></details> | 21 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Zooming without Zooming v2: From Seeing Details to Connecting Evidence for Generalizable Multimodal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37037v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal perception forms the foundation of multimodal intelligence. In particular, spatial perception and multimodal long-context understanding are two pivotal tasks that underpin embodied AI and multimodal agents. However, synthesizing targeted, high-quality training data for these tasks remains difficult and costly. Our key insight is that these tasks share an underlying capability, which we call connecting evidence: identifying relevant visual facts, binding them to the correct entities, recovering their relations, and composing them into an answer. Crucially, targeted training data are far easier to synthesize for this capability than for these tasks themselves. To strengthen this capability, we propose EvidenceConnect, an easy-to-implement data synthesis framework that uses zoomed views of related regions within a single image to generate questions requiring comparison, aggregation, or chaining of visual evidence. To ensure quality, a reference answer is retained only when independent VLMs agree on it, and region-ablation tests discard questions whose answer survives the removal of selected regions. We then train base VLMs on the resulting data with RLVR, obtaining Zooming without Zooming v2 (ZwZ2). Although the synthesized data are not tailored to any downstream benchmark, ZwZ2 improves fine-grained and spatial perception, with further gains on embodied spatial reasoning, multimodal long-context understanding, multimodal agent tasks, and general visual reasoning. These results establish EvidenceConnect as an effective framework for supervising evidence connection and suggest that strengthening this shared capability leads to more generalizable multimodal perception.</p></details> |  |
| **[AmaraSpatial-10K: A Spatially and Semantically Aligned 3D Dataset for Spatial Computing and Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23018v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web-scale 3D asset collections are abundant but rarely ready to ingest without cleanup, suffering from arbitrary metric scaling, incorrect pivots, brittle geometry, and incomplete textures, defects that limit their use in embodied AI, robotics, and spatial computing. We present AmaraSpatial-10K, a dataset of 10,071 synthetic 3D assets standardised for direct ingestion. Each asset ships as a metric-scaled, deterministically anchored .glb with separated PBR maps, a convex collision hull, a paired reference image, and multi-sentence text metadata. Alongside the dataset we introduce a reusable evaluation suite for 3D asset banks, a continuous Scale Plausibility Score (SPS), a keyword-based Concept Density metric, anchor-error auditing, and a cross-modal CLIP coherence protocol, and apply it to AmaraSpatial-10K alongside matched subsets of Objaverse, HSSD, ABO, and GSO. We check the annotations independently of the LLM/VLM pipeline that produced them: measured ABO object heights fall inside our scale intervals 88.7-90.7% of the time, and three non-author raters auditing a stratified sample of 314 assets judge 95.5% plausibly scaled and 99.7% correctly anchored. AmaraSpatial-10K improves CLIP Recall@5 by 3.4x over Objaverse (0.612 vs. 0.181, median rank 267 -> 3), achieves a 99.1% settling-stability rate under Habitat-Sim in static placement and collision tests, with ~20x lower per-load wall time, and as a drop-in Holodeck asset bank improves planned-scale matching and is preferred by blinded human raters in 27 of 40 paired scenes. Controlled ablations on the same asset bank attribute the retrieval gain to description richness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track)</p></details> |
| **[Closing the empirical loop: autonomous AI agents conduct end-to-end research with human participants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13421v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence systems are transforming scientific discovery by accelerating specific research tasks, from protein structure prediction to materials design, yet remain confined to narrow domains requiring substantial human oversight. Exponential growth of scientific literature and increasing domain specialisation constrain researchers capacity to synthesise knowledge across disciplines and develop unifying theories, motivating exploration of more general-purpose AI systems for science. Here we show that a domain-agnostic, agentic AI system can independently navigate the scientific workflow, from hypothesis generation through data collection to manuscript preparation. The system autonomously designed and executed three psychological studies on visual working memory, mental rotation, and imagery vividness, executed online data collection with 288 participants, developed analysis pipelines through over 8-hour continuous coding sessions, and produced completed manuscripts. The results demonstrate the capability of AI scientific discovery pipelines to conduct research with theoretical reasoning and methodological rigour comparable to experienced researchers, though with limitations in conceptual nuance and theoretical interpretation. This is a step toward embodied AI that can test hypotheses through real-world experiments, accelerating discovery by autonomously exploring regions of scientific space that human cognitive and resource constraints might otherwise leave unexplored. It raises important questions about the nature of scientific understanding and attribution of scientific credit.</p></details> |  |
| **[EAST: Embodied AI Safety Testing via Systematic Fault Injection in Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34358v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Task success is not a sufficient measure of safety for embodied artificial intelligence (AI), because robots may complete objectives while violating safety requirements. Existing evaluations often emphasize performance and robustness, but lack a systematic route from documented safety hazards to executable, verifiable tests. We present EAST (Embodied AI Safety Testing), a digital-twin framework that converts literature-grounded safety evidence into fault-injection tests across perception, instructions, environments, actions, and timing. EAST combines an expert-reviewed safety-fault taxonomy and eight executable operators with applicability and validity constraints. Its three-stage workflow generates test specifications, verifies fault realization during simulated execution, and independently assesses safety challenges, violations, task outcomes, and behavioral signatures using simulator-state evidence. The framework preserves execution traces and replayable records, supporting attribution and reproducibility across experiments. We evaluate EAST on six RoboTwin manipulation tasks and two policy architectures, ACT and RDT, against alternative testing strategies under matched budgets. Across five comparative campaigns, EAST targets all 74 admissible task-policy-operator combinations and obtains valid evidence for 63, while achieving safety-challenge exposure rates close to Random-Legal and Rule-Based. Moreover, 82 valid EAST executions complete their tasks despite safety violations, revealing risks concealed by success-oriented evaluation. These findings support systematic, validity-aware, reproducible safety testing beyond task-completion metrics.</p></details> |  |
| **[3D Generation for Embodied AI and Robotic Simulation: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26509v4)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and robotic systems increasingly depend on scalable, diverse, and physically grounded 3D content for simulation-based training and real-world deployment. While 3D generative modeling has advanced rapidly, embodied applications impose requirements far beyond visual realism: generated objects must carry kinematic structure and material properties, scenes must support interaction and task execution, and the resulting content must bridge the gap between simulation and reality. This survey reviews 3D generation for embodied AI and organizes the literature around three roles that 3D generation plays in embodied systems. In Data Generator, 3D generation produces simulation-ready objects and assets, including articulated, physically grounded, and deformable content for downstream interaction; in Simulation Environments, it constructs interactive and task-oriented worlds, spanning structure-aware, controllable, and agentic scene generation; and in Sim2Real Bridge, it supports digital twin reconstruction, data augmentation, and synthetic demonstrations for downstream robot learning and real-world transfer. We also show that the field is shifting from visual realism toward interaction readiness, and we identify the main bottlenecks, including limited physical annotations, the gap between geometric quality and physical validity, fragmented evaluation, and the persistent sim-to-real divide, that must be addressed for 3D generation to become a dependable foundation for embodied intelligence. | **[rMuscle: Robotic Muscle Memory for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19104v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Factory work is a promising early scenario for embodied AI: assigning repetitive manual jobs to robots has clear economic payoff, and a structured station keeps the jobs tractable for current policies. We further find that such similarity extends beyond observations and action trajectories to internal model states. Drawing on these observations, we present rMuscle, a real-time VLA inference framework inspired by human muscle memory. A harness plans action stages over this state, invokes experts via their native conditioning interfaces, and writes each action's effects back, so that every invocation is grounded in the evolving world. To reduce physical artifacts, bounded residual optimization and contact-driven physics simulation further ensure the physical plausibility of human motion and object manipulation, respectively, while preserving interaction intent. | **[Physical AI: Concepts, Techniques, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27045v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, physical artificial intelligence (physical AI) has experienced rapid development, capturing significant attention in academia and industry. However, the field has yet to reach consensus on its definition, technical landscape, and core challenges. To address this gap, this monograph seeks to define physical AI, present its technical landscape, and examine each key technical subfield in depth. In doing so, it establishes a unified framework for Physical AI: artificial systems that perceive, model, and act upon the physical world through continuous feedback under real-world constraints. Further, this monograph distinguishes physical AI from conventional digital AI, embodied AI, cyber-physical systems, and digital twins. It develops the foundations of physical AI across physics, control, cognition, learning, and reasoning. It reviews key methodologies, including multimodal perception, physical data and simulation, world models, embodied foundation models, motor-control systems, multi-agent coordination, and hardware-software co-design. Furthermore, it examines applications spanning robotics, manufacturing, infrastructure, scientific experimentation, and autonomous services. Particular attention is given to sim-to-real transfer, reliability, real-time execution, evaluation, safety, governance, and long-term deployment. By connecting perception, prediction, decision-making, action, and adaptation in a closed loop, this monograph identifies the technical challenges and research directions required to advance from narrow automation toward more generalizable physical AI and, ultimately, physical artificial general intelligence (Physical AGI).</p></details> | <details><summary>Monog...</summary><p>Monograph, 12 chapters, 388 pages, 64 figures</p></details> |
| **[ForagerSim: Evaluating Embodied Map-Based Reasoning in Large-Scale Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26395v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Animals build cognitive maps of vast environments, use them to perform map-based navigation, and might even be reusing the same circuitry used in spatial reasoning for creating and using maps of arbitrary task-spaces. To address this, we present ForagerSim --- an interdisciplinary research platform and simulation environment focused on embodied tasks that require map-based reasoning, with scenarios ranging from mobile-robot search and rescue to animal foraging in both complex mazes and vast, potentially infinite open areas. ForagerSim is highly modular and allows modifying independent axes of difficulty related to perception (abstract 2D sensing to full RGB or LiDAR), scale (room-scale to multi-km scale), topology (open-fields to complex mazes), dynamics (smoke, debris, sensorimotor failures and others) and tasks (from simple 2D coverage to foraging-inspired well-alternation tasks). | **[Compile Once, Run Cheap: Frozen, Portable Skills for Embodied Agents, and What Recursive Self-Improvement Adds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25114v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI lacks the data-rich pretraining that gave language models their agents and skills, so new manipulation capability costs demonstrations per task or a frontier model in every episode. Planning toward this goal is then organized as a graph of rule-specific nodes, where higher-level nodes inherit the union of their parents' rules, and candidate plans are progressively verified and aggregated until a single plan satisfies every relevant rule. These results guide joint design of VLA model architectures, hardware, and runtime policies.</p></details> | <details><summary>To ap...</summary><p>To appear in the Proceedings of the 32nd ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems (ASPLOS '27). 17 pages, 14 figures</p></details> |

Our evaluations show that current methods remain challenged by the dense object arrangements, occlusions, limited free space, and constrained interaction regions found in realistic lived-in homes. | **[Generative AI for Autonomous Driving: Frontiers and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08854v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) constitutes a transformative technological wave that reconfigures industries through its unparalleled capabilities for content creation, reasoning, planning, and multimodal understanding. Through examples across autonomous vehicles, industrial automation, healthcare robotics, and humanoid systems, we identify significant performance improvements and summarize promising research directions in data-efficient learning, sim-to-real transfer, edge-compatible architectures, and safety frameworks. ## Reinforcement Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Video-Coder: Reinforcing Agentic Coding for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09894v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent years have witnessed remarkable progress in video generation in terms of visual quality and temporal consistency. However, generating videos with precise spatial structure, coherent motion dynamics, and controllable scene composition remains challenging. In this paper, we introduce Video-Coder, the first attempt to train a code-driven video generation agent, which autonomously writes and executes code to construct a dynamic 3D scene (Draft Video) that provides structure and motion signals to downstream video generators. To support this paradigm, we design a dedicated data pipeline and curate two high-quality datasets, Video-Coder-SFT-6k and Video-Coder-RL-2k, which contain diverse prompts and ground-truth reference videos. We further develop the Video- Coder Harness, a unified interactive environment integrating code execution, 3D scene rendering, and execution feedback for agent training and inference. We then train Video-Coder with SFT fol- lowed by harness-native agentic reinforcement learning with dual reward feedback, which combines execution-based and video-based rewards. Additionally, we design an annealing training strategy that gradually reduces the execution reward over time, allowing the model to rely more on the video reward in the later training stages. Experiments show that Video-Coder achieves significant gains, improving Minimax-H3 by approximately 7 points on PhyGenBench and 24 points on VBench-2.0. We hope this work can serve as an open foundation for coding agents in video generation, and we fully open-source our data, models, and code</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page:https://cywang735.github.io/Video-Coder/ Code: https://github.com/CYWang735/Video-Coder</p></details> |
| **[On the Reliability of Majority-Vote Pseudo-Labels in Test-Time Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09872v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) adapts pretrained language models to unlabeled target-domain data using self-generated pseudo-labels, whose reliability remains poorly understood. We analyze majority-vote supervision for TTRL with online Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and decompose its error into population-majority bias and prompt-dependent finite-rollout variation. Because population-majority bias is generally unidentifiable without external supervision, we focus on mitigating the downstream effects of finite-rollout variation. We propose \emph{Prompt-Conditional Distributionally Robust DPO (PC-Dr.DPO)}, which conditions within-prompt robust reweighting on observed vote reliability to make updates from sampling-sensitive prompts more conservative. We further introduce a cache-based mechanism to reduce self-supervised forgetting during online adaptation. Across six mathematical and knowledge-intensive reasoning benchmarks and five model backbones, PC-Dr.DPO matches or outperforms the strongest TTRL baseline in 24 of 30 settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Grasp-TAGS: Task-Aware Grasp Selection via Critic Distillation for Prehensile Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09864v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Loco-manipulation, which requires mobile manipulators to coordinate locomotion and manipulation over long horizons, is a key capability for large-object rearrangement in applications such as service robotics and warehouse automation. Existing approaches address this challenge either through model-based methods, which often require accurate dynamics models, or through learning-based methods, which reduce this requirement but typically assume a fixed or predefined initial grasp. However, different geometrically stable grasps can lead to substantially different downstream manipulation performance. Therefore, we propose Grasp-TAGS, a task-aware grasp selection framework for goal-conditioned large-object rearrangement. We train a unified PPO-based reinforcement learning policy across multiple initial grasp configurations and distill the learned critic into a lightweight grasp utility network that ranks candidate grasps using only the candidate grasp configuration and goal information. At inference time, we select the grasp with the highest predicted downstream utility for the downstream policy to complete the task, before even making a contact. Experiments demonstrate that Grasp-TAGS improves rearrangement success over both fixed single-grasp policies and random grasp selection, achieving an average success rate of 89.84% compared with 79.87% for the best-performing single-grasp policies, an improvement of 9.97 percentage points. Grasp-TAGS also reduces final orientation errors across all evaluated objects. | **[BRAVR: An AP-Assisted Online DRL Mechanism for Interactive VR Bitrate Adaptation over Wi-Fi](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24389v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive virtual reality (VR) streaming over Wi-Fi requires stringent latency and reliability guarantees, which become increasingly difficult to achieve under dynamic channel conditions and shared-medium contention. It also outperforms the AP-blind ablation, demonstrating the value of incorporating network-level information into the bitrate adaptation control loop. Overall, these results demonstrate the potential of AP-assisted online learning for decentralized interactive VR streaming over commodity Wi-Fi and provide practical insights into bitrate adaptation in shared wireless environments.</p></details> |  |

To address this problem, we introduce Visual Learned Distance (VLD), a scalable framework for image-goal navigation that decouples perception learning from policy learning. We first train a self-supervised distance-to-goal predictor on about 3,000 hours of internet-scale and simulated video, providing a distance signal that a reinforcement learning (RL) policy can minimize. The RL policy is trained separately in simulation using privileged geometric distances perturbed by GeoNoise, a learned noise model that reproduces the viewpoint- and overlap-dependent errors of the trained distance predictor. At deployment, the policy consumes VLD predictions zero-shot, without retraining, inheriting ``where to go'' from large-scale visual training while retaining the robust navigation behaviors learned in simulation. This error modeling is key to the transfer: with VLD inputs, GeoNoise-trained policies reach $58.7\%$ success on HM3D and $76.2\%$ on Gibson, whereas policies trained with generic temporally correlated noise collapse to $4.9\%$ and $11.5\%$. On a physical robot, the VLD-driven policy succeeds in $88\%$ of trials, compared with $60\%$ for a strong end-to-end image-goal baseline that outperforms it in simulation. | **[RPEG: Training Reasoning Language Models Scalably and Cost-Effectively](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09207v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) is now central to training Reasoning Language Models (RLMs), but RLM pipelines are difficult to scale and costly to run because each RL step couples multiple large models, heterogeneous stages, cross-step state, and complex asynchronous dependencies. Across systems with up to 256 GH200 GPUs, our distributed runtime engine implementing RPEG improves performance by up to 2x over ReaL and reduces step latency by up to 2.48x over verl, while increasing hardware utilization by up to 30%. Overall, RPEG provides a graph-driven foundation for next-generation training pipelines for frontier reasoning LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[TailSieve: Partial-Rollout-Guided Tail Routing for LLM Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22788v3)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large-scale rollouts have become a core component of modern LLM systems, spanning reinforcement learning (RL) post-training, on-policy distillation, and sampling-heavy evaluation. Motivated by the observed persistence of prompt-level tail tendencies across policy updates, TailSieve uses partial-rollout completion status as a training-free signal for identifying candidate tail groups. Selected prompts are combined with newly sampled prompts, and all responses are generated from scratch under the current policy. A hierarchical controller uses response-work history and a serving model: a fast inner loop adjusts the number of isolated groups to balance completion times, while a slower outer loop adapts how many replicas serve these groups. Across the evaluated configurations, TailSieve achieves up to $1.67\times$ speedup with routing alone and $2.59\times$ with routing and speculation combined, both over uniform group routing.</p></details> |  |
| **[AdaFloor: Adaptive Floor Planning and Rank-Time Reuse for Long-Tail MoE RL Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06984v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synchronous MoE reinforcement-learning post-training suffers from response-length imbalance. AdaFloor converts this EP-coupled capacity into bounded leases for auxiliary work to improve system resource efficiency through rank-time reuse. Historical prompt-tail prediction and a calibrated guard construct the KV-safe layout. AdaFloor then selects the floor-specific safe quorum schedule with the largest predicted release area. Its runtime shrinks the EP group and leases detached ranks until full-world restoration. Across three updated-policy seeds on DeepScaleR mathematical reasoning rollouts, its capacity-oriented Natural floor2 configuration with TailGuard reduces Vanilla's 29393.9 rank-seconds of dummy run by 96.15%. Rollout shortening eliminates 18693.6 rank-seconds, while coordinated exits convert another 9568.6 rank-seconds into reusable capacity, leaving 1131.7 rank-seconds. For sidecar reuse, its Planned residency floor4 configuration without TailGuard serves 3134 Qwen3-8B GSM8K queries per epoch at 99.46% accuracy and incurs only a 2 to 3% primary throughput reduction against the same configuration with sidecar execution disabled. Its latency-oriented Planned residency floor4 configuration with TailGuard reduces rollout time by 24.09% relative to Vanilla and by 2.31% relative to LengthSort with TailGuard. | **[Safe Reinforcement Learning via Probabilistic Option Shielding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09197v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Shielding is a safe reinforcement learning technique that provides safety guarantees while learning, by blocking unsafe actions. | **[CARGO: Causal Attribution and Responsibility-Guided Preventive Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Cooperative Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09139v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study presents Causal Attribution and Responsibility-Guided Optimization (CARGO), a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for cooperative on-ramp merging in mixed traffic. A shared latent-context posterior and an action-conditioned response model estimate directed behavioral influence, with posterior uncertainty propagated into interaction-edge selection. Experiments in highway-env across three traffic densities, together with controlled graph-recovery and attribution studies, support the effectiveness of the framework. | **[TUGB: A Two-stage Uncertainty-aware Generative Auto-Bidding Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09091v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative modeling, particularly Decision Transformers, shows promise in auto-bidding strategies. Experiments show that \ours~consistently outperforms strong retrieval-based and DCI baselines, while demonstrating robust cross-lingual generalization and substantially reducing redundant corpus interactions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Grounding-Aware Judge Reward Reinforcement Learning for Out-of-Domain Face Anti-Spoofing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09047v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Face Anti-Spoofing (FAS) must distinguish genuine faces from presentation attacks. A unified Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) is an attractive solution: it detects physical attacks (print, replay, mask) in one model and produces interpretable forensic evidence via Chain-of-Thought (CoT). A central obstacle to post-training such a model with reinforcement learning is that FAS reasoning has no rule-verifiable ground truth: only the binary verdict is checkable, while the reasoning chain cannot be verified by any hand-crafted rule. Optimizing the verdict alone is therefore a sparse, hackable signal. The model can reach the correct answer through a shortcut such as a false-rejection bias, which does not generalize. We address this with a grounding-aware judge reward. A 32B vision-language judge scores each reasoning chain against the image on evidence anchoring, logical coherence, and domain knowledge, turning the image itself into the verification signal. The reward is applied in a Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) stage after CoT supervised fine-tuning. It is the judge, rather than the RL objective, that supplies the process supervision a verdict-only reward cannot. Our study starts from a model that already performs well In-Domain (ID) after CoT-based supervised fine-tuning and asks how to improve its Out-of-Domain (OOD) generalization. We find that grounding-aware rewards are an effective means to that end. We validate the method on OOD generalization, where the reward reduces ACER on two physical benchmarks (LCC-FASD 0.288 to 0.273, MSU-MFSD 0.431 to 0.409) while preserving ID accuracy (CelebA-Spoof 0.033 to 0.035). Against ResNet-50/101 and CLIP-ViT-B/16 baselines, the MLLM dominates ID and matches or surpasses the strongest baseline OOD. To our knowledge this is the first use of LLM-as-judge process supervision for FAS.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Robust Reinforcement Learning Market Making: Lessons from Loss Function Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09012v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Market makers play a vital role in ensuring efficient trade within financial markets. Our results underscore the significance of striking a balance between profit maximization and risk management in algorithmic trading, providing actionable insights for refining strategies in practical settings.</p></details> | 6 pages, 3 figures |
| **[FASTFUNNELS: Agile Multi Robot Navigation in Narrow Spaces via Adaptive Dynamic Funnels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09005v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We focus on the problem of high-speed multi-robot navigation in cluttered spaces with tight spatial constraints. A key challenge is balancing safety with agility, as existing methods often trade one for the other (slowing down near a bottleneck to avoid collisions versus failing to swerve in time at high speeds), especially for car-like systems. Our approach belongs to the family of formation control methods, in which Ackermann-steering agents coordinate while navigating tight spaces. However, current formation control techniques rely on rigid, pre-defined shapes and transitions that depend on initial conditions, the environment, and vehicle dynamics, and are impractical to maintain at high speeds. For example, agents passing through a small gap must first form a square, transform into a line using analytical equations, and then return to a square. In contrast, our approach is inspired by a droplet of water that continuously adapts its shape to the space it flows through without slowing down. We propose a hierarchical algorithm in which, at the high level, a reinforcement learning policy produces a dynamic, blob-like elliptical patch that morphs its shape in real time, squeezing, stretching, and orienting itself around obstacles and through tight spaces. At the low level, the robots are controlled via distributed model predictive control (DMPC) to stay inside the patch, which acts as a soft forcing function, while avoiding collisions with each other. We evaluate our approach, FastFunnels, on simulated F1TENTH racing cars across 14 scenarios with N=1 to N=6 robots, averaged over 20 trials each. FastFunnels achieves an 89% completion rate at 7.73 m/s, while all baselines either fail to complete the track or succeed only at very low speeds (0.07-1.79 m/s, about 40% completion rate).</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 4 figures, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[Sharing the Road: LLM-Based Bargaining in Mixed-Autonomy Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09004v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mixed-autonomy traffic involves heterogeneous driving populations competing for shared road space. Previous studies have shown the existence and emergence of collective rationality using game-theoretic and reinforcement learning approaches, which rely on explicitly specified behavioral assumptions or reward functions for driving agents. However, how bargaining behaviors between driving agents evolve and lead to collective outcomes based on richer agent characteristics and language-based reasoning remains less understood. To fill this gap, we develop a bargaining framework based on large language models (LLMs) in which population-level autonomous vehicle (AV) and human-driven vehicle (HV) agents negotiate road-space allocations under heterogeneous objectives and private information. Through controlled experiments varying traffic composition, agent objectives, and traffic-domain knowledge, we examine bargaining behavior, coordination, and resource utilization. Results show that negotiated allocations respond to relative traffic density without strictly following proportional sharing, while density-balanced states exhibit greater coordination difficulty. Fairness-oriented objectives substantially improve agreement rates but do not necessarily yield equally high road-space utilization. Furthermore, domain knowledge affects bargaining outcomes differently across objective configurations, shaping both coordination and the role-dependent effects of fairness. These findings demonstrate the potential of LLM-based bargaining for modeling and designing interactions among driving agents in mixed-autonomy traffic.</p></details> |  |

Each prefix is replayed in the environment to recover a valid intermediate state, after which the student continues online interaction and receives task reward. Unlike response-only GRPO, Prefix-GRPO also applies clipped policy updates to historical assistant tokens inside the replayed prefix, using a policy-distilled SFT checkpoint to estimate their old log-probabilities. This unifies prefix learning and continuation learning within the same policy-optimization form. Experiments on TextCraft, BabyAI, and ALFWorld show that Prefix-GRPO improves small-model agents over distillation and standard RL baselines, while ablations show that replay alone is insufficient without explicit prefix-token optimization. | **[Improving Generalization Robustness of Multimodal RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08802v3)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) makes Multimodal Large Language Models more accurate, but the gains are brittle: simply paraphrasing a question or changing the prompt template can degrade them, which challenges reliable deployment in high-stakes scenarios like medical VQA. Second, the training distribution covers only a thin slice of the real-world prompts that the model might meet at deployment, so policies that perform well on the training distribution can behave differently under unseen prompts during test. Both failures call for a robust post-training method that helps the policy cover a broader distribution of semantically equivalent prompts, and we identify two measures that help achieve this objective: separating format from semantics in the reward, and applying policy invariance across perturbed prompts with equivalent semantics. We therefore propose Prompt-Invariant RLVR (PIRL), consisting of a dynamic trinary reward and a consistency regularizer based on an embedding-space adversary. Under stress testing, PIRL's average accuracy on benchmarks drops by only $\le 1\%$, where GRPO drops ~3%. We introduce reward budgeting, a plug-in advantage construction assigning a fixed total reward budget to each prompt. For binary rewards and independent on-policy rollouts, its expected unclipped score-function update is the exact gradient of a finite-$G$ concave objective with diminishing returns; its advantage gap decreases as more rollouts become correct. On Qwen3-8B and Llama3.2-3B-Instruct, reward budgeting consistently improves RL baselines, with gains up to +12.50 on AMC23 and +10.00 on AIME24 and AIME25. These results support an update-dynamics view of premature convergence and substantial gains without explicitly preserving entropy. Code is available at https://github.com/Qwen-Applications/RewardBudget.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

| **[GeoJeV: Unified Spatial Probability for Training-Free Efficient Evidence Acquisition in Ultra-High-Resolution Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08879v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing imagery provides rich spatial details but poses challenges for accurate and efficient multimodal understanding. To learn efficiently from this reward, we use the off-policy RL algorithm XQC and combine a high gain setting for the joint position controllers with left-right symmetry augmentation. | **[Counterfactual Distillation: Internalizing Reflective Experience into LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16843v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models increasingly act as autonomous agents in long-horizon interactions, learning from final outcomes and environmental feedback. However, optimizing from such interaction experience remains challenging, as feedback is heterogeneous and rarely carries explicit reward information. Existing post-training approaches rely on outcome-driven methods, such as reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), which primarily exploit final success signals while leaving interaction experience and feedback underutilized. In sparse-reward, long-horizon tasks, this often results in distribution sharpening: the policy reinforces a narrow set of already-successful behaviors, without substantially improving the feedback-grounded agency needed for broader problem-solving capacity (e.g., Pass@k). We propose Counterfactual Distillation, a framework that converts exploration-derived experience into trainable policy supervision. Specifically, we organize exploration as a tree-structured search process, where the agent reflects on past decision points and makes new experience-guided decisions to form alternative branches. These corrections are then distilled as counterfactual targets: we train the model to predict the revised action from the original history without explicitly providing the experience, thereby introducing out-of-distribution behavior patterns and expanding exploration capacity. Across diverse interactive coding and agentic tasks, our method outperforms outcome-driven baselines such as GRPO and experience-based methods such as Early Experience, with gains of up to 14 percentage points over the base model on Pass@128. | **[Locating Contact on Tendon-Driven Continuum Robots From Tendon Measurements Alone via Constrained Moving Horizon Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09877v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tendon-driven continuum robots can reach through constrained spaces, but contact along their bodies is invisible to the sensors at their motors. We show that this is a structural limitation rather than a tuning problem: the map from contact forces to tendon measurements has a null space of dimension at least seven, so no estimator that leaves the contact support free can locate contact. When the obstacle geometry is known, however, non-penetration and complementarity make the contact force locally identifiable, and we prove that the resulting system with unknown contact inputs is locally exponentially strongly detectable. We present a constrained moving horizon estimator (MHE) that encodes these conditions and runs in real time as one quadratic program per sample, and we give a local, mode-wise robust stability result for it. In a finite- element benchmark of a two-section robot pressing and sliding on a wall, the estimator located contact to 0.9 mm and estimated contact force to 0.22 N, compared with 65 mm and 0.72 N for a tuned extended Kalman filter (EKF) with state projection. The estimator tolerated millimeter- level errors in the obstacle position and unmodeled wall friction, under which the projected EKF failed. Under randomized model mismatch over 40 trials, including unmodeled tendon friction, it remained better in force and location in every trial. Without contact, the MHE offered no advantage over the EKF, which confirms that its benefit comes from the contact constraints. This report extends a letter submitted to IEEE Control Systems Letters with complete proofs and additional experiments.</p></details> |  |

We propose failure-boundary profiles, an evaluation in which each scenario family varies one measured factor, such as corridor width or the time gap in oncoming traffic, and each variant is certified from measurement as one where the agent should proceed or one where it should stop. Along this axis we estimate two curves per agent, commitment (the probability of attempting the passage) and competence (the probability of passing without contact), and locate where each crosses one half, with intervals that account for the simulator's run-to-run variance. The certified labels also give a summary metric, decision accuracy, which credits clean passes on passable variants and stops on blocked ones. We evaluate seven driving agents on seven CARLA families. The rule-based expert reaches a decision accuracy of 0.74 and TFv6, the highest-scoring learned agent, 0.60. Agents with similar driving scores fail in different ways, and agents that reliably stop at a blocked corridor still rarely wait for a gap in oncoming traffic.
