We propose PACE (Phase-Aware Chunk Execution), a training-free method that selects execution horizons online from predicted action chunks. PACE uses prominent low-speed valleys in the predicted speed profile as candidate replanning boundaries, exploiting the phase-dependent kinematics of manipulation trajectories. By default, PACE calibrates the valley threshold from each task's existing training demonstrations, without task-specific fixed-horizon tuning. On the 50-task RoboTwin2.0 benchmark, PACE improves average success from 57.8% to 64.2% over the strongest fixed-horizon baseline and outperforms the adaptive execution baselines AAC and AutoHorizon. With GR00T N1.7 on LIBERO, PACE achieves the highest overall success rate among the evaluated strategies, supporting its applicability across base policies, benchmarks, and action representations. On real robots, PACE improves the average task score from 60.7 to 77.7 and success rate from 50.7% to 70.4% over full-chunk execution. Code is available at https://niejunnan25.github.io/pace-project/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 33 pages including appendices and the paper checklist. Updated experiments, discussion, references, and author list</p></details> |
| **[Capability-Aware Heterogeneous Control Barrier Functions for Decentralized Multi-Robot Safe Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13245v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe decentralized navigation in heterogeneous robot teams is challenging because robots with different dynamics and control limits cannot satisfy identical collision-avoidance obligations equally well. Existing methods often rely on model-specific safety formulations or allocate avoidance responsibility without considering whether the assigned control is physically realizable, possibly leading to safety-constrained QP infeasibility and safety violations. We propose Capability-Aware Heterogeneous Control Barrier Functions (CA-HCBF), a decentralized framework for consistent safety enforcement and capability-aware responsibility allocation. We derive a unified operational-space second-order control-affine formulation for holonomic and nonholonomic mobile robots through canonical transformation and backstepping, addressing the relative-degree mismatch while preserving forward invariance of the pairwise safe set. Second, we quantify each robot's achievable control authority along the safety-critical direction using a support-function-based capability metric and use it to distribute pairwise avoidance responsibility. A feasibility-aware clipping mechanism further mitigates physically unrealizable responsibility assignments. Simulations with up to 30 heterogeneous robots and hardware experiments demonstrate high task completion with low safety violations and reduction in local QP infeasibility.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 3 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[Clinician-AI Collaboration in Endovascular Neurointerventions: In-Vitro User Study of Copilot Assistance Modes for Robotic Navigation Across the Autonomy Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03446v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mechanical thrombectomy (MT) is an effective treatment for acute ischemic stroke, yet access remains limited by the availability of neurointerventional expertise. Telerobotic and AI-assisted systems may help address this challenge, but it remains unclear how clinicians interact with different levels of autonomy and how autonomous navigation systems perform in realistic environments. This work introduces Copilot Assistance Modes (CAMs), a framework for clinician-AI collaboration in robotic endovascular navigation spanning the autonomy spectrum from manual operation to full autonomy. In the first in-vitro user study comparing clinician-AI collaboration strategies, 11 experts and two students evaluated six navigation modes in a patient-specific vascular phantom. Objective performance metrics were combined with subjective assessments, including workload and usability. Autonomous performance was additionally evaluated both in silico and in vitro to investigate sim-to-real transfer. Hands-on manual navigation achieved the strongest overall performance (100% success). While fully autonomous navigation achieved 81% success in simulation, performance dropped to 23% in vitro. In contrast, several clinician-AI shared-autonomy modes achieved success rates of 92-97%, approximately four times higher than full autonomy and comparable to direct robotic control (95%), demonstrating mitigation of sim-to-real domain shift through clinician-AI collaboration. Among the evaluated AI-driven approaches, user-directed task delegation and rotational shared control demonstrated the most favorable balance between performance and clinician acceptance. These findings suggest that effective clinician-AI collaboration and allocation of control authority may currently be more important for reliable robotic neurointerventional navigation than increasing autonomy alone.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 10 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[CRONOS: Benchmarking Non-Episodic Reinforcement Learning for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05080v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) is promising for adapting robot policies to unstructured real-world environments. However, standard RL pipelines rely on episodic training with frequent scene resets, which is impractical for real-world deployment due to the need for substantial human intervention. While prior work has studied non-episodic RL in relatively small policies trained from scratch, its behavior when fine-tuning modern large-scale pre-trained vision-language-action (VLA) models remains underexplored. In this work, we introduce CRONOS, a simulation benchmark for RL fine-tuning of VLAs under non-episodic scene resets. CRONOS leverages high-fidelity physics simulators and adopts shared-scene multi-task settings. Using CRONOS, we show that naively fine-tuning pre-trained policies fails in non-episodic settings due to shifts in object configurations and end-effector poses; however, these challenges can be mitigated through targeted reset allocation and by addressing biases in pre-trained models. Moreover, we find that non-episodic training enhances long-horizon manipulation and improves generalization to previously unseen object configurations and task sequences. Project page: https://embodiedai-ntu.github.io/cronos/</p></details> |  |
| **[Nested Field-of-View Barriers for Teleoperated Multi-Robot Relay Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05074v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teleoperation of mobile robots in communication-denied environments requires maintaining a communication link while allowing the operator to maneuver freely. We propose a shared-autonomy framework in which a teleoperated leader is followed by a chain of autonomous robots that relays a line of sight to a fixed base station, a geometric proxy for the link. Connectivity is enforced geometrically through visibility-based Control Barrier Functions (CBFs), with each agent computing its control input locally through a quadratic program. A strict visibility constraint guarantees connectivity, but a follower with no independent motion objective remains stationary, stalling the leader at the boundary of the safe set. Relaxing it preserves mobility at the cost of the guarantee. Our framework resolves this conflict through nested hard-soft visibility constraints: a full field-of-view (FoV) constraint enforced strictly to guarantee connectivity and a contracted FoV constraint imposed on each follower as a relaxed behavioral objective that drives it inward before the connectivity boundary is reached. We prove feasibility of the local quadratic programs and forward invariance of the connected, collision-free set under the resulting distributed controller. The contraction parameter shapes follower behavior without affecting the guarantee, which follows from the strict constraint alone. The framework is evaluated in confined and cluttered environments on two simulated systems: a kinematic integrator and software-in-the-loop Crazyflie quadrotors. Compared with a baseline tuned to reach the goal in comparable time, the framework keeps the links far closer to the connected set and moves the followers less.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 4 figures, 1 table. Submitted to the American Control Conference (ACC)</p></details> |
| **[Haptic Shared Control for Multi-Robot Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05027v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems offer significant advantages in terms of flexibility and redundancy, enabling the efficient distribution of complex tasks among multiple robots. However, their deployment requires advanced control strategies to ensure optimal cooperation between autonomous agents and human operators. We propose a modification of the global connectivity maintenance algorithm to incorporate measurements taken in agent's local frame, using sensors with limited fields of view. This algorithm, responsible for the autonomous behavior of the multi-robot system, is combined with different adaptive supervision strategies, allowing the user to exert control at varying scales over the entire group. (i) Single Agent Control (S): The operator directly controls a single robot, (ii) Pinning Control (P): The operator imposes constraints on multiple robots to influence the group, (iii) Centroid Control (C): The operator acts on the system's center of mass to steer the entire fleet. In conjunction with the connectivity maintenance algorithm, these methods ensure compliance with inter-agent constraints while providing haptic feedback to the user regarding the system's connectivity state. The effectiveness of these approaches was evaluated through a user study, comparing performance with and without haptic feedback across multiple scenarios tailored to the different control modes.</p></details> | <details><summary>PhD t...</summary><p>PhD thesis, Université de Rennes (IRISA UMR 6074 / Inria Rennes), defended 30 April 2025. 134 pages, 38 figures</p></details> |
| **[For You, Not for Everyone: Bystander-Aware Delivery of Critical Feedback in Robot Tutoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05001v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robots in shared settings may expose information intended for an individual user to nearby bystanders. Robot tutoring makes this concern particularly salient: critical feedback is directed at an individual learner, yet bystanders may overhear or see it. To address this, we compared context-invariant and context-adaptive robot behaviors in a between-subjects study with 40 participants. Following an initial phase without a bystander, a bystander entered the setting. Participants then received two scripted critical feedback messages concerning their pronunciation and handwriting, independent of their actual performance. With the context-invariant behavior, the robot tutor delivered both messages aloud. With the context-adaptive behavior, the robot tutor scanned for the bystander, turned its body and display away after detecting them, gave a spoken attention cue, and presented the same feedback messages silently on its display. Participants rated the robot tutor higher in perceived privacy awareness and perceived social competence in the context-adaptive condition than in the context-invariant condition, while evidence for reduced experienced embarrassment was inconclusive. These findings inform the design and evaluation of robots that respond to bystander presence by adapting how they communicate potentially sensitive information.</p></details> | Submitted for Review |
| **[ResponsibleRobotBench: Benchmarking Risk-Aware Task-Level Decision Making for Robotic Manipulation with Large Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04308v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe task-level manipulation requires a planner to recognize hazards, select appropriate mitigation actions, and satisfy their execution prerequisites without abandoning the original goal. We introduce ResponsibleRobotBench, a benchmark of 23 manipulation task configurations with separately specified goals and safety conditions. It connects hazard-category predictions, planned actions, execution feedback, and terminal outcomes at the episode level. Across evaluations of five large multimodal models, terminal safety and safe task completion can diverge: defense prompts increase terminal safety for three tested models but affect safe success in different directions. Correct hazard-category predictions also fail to ensure executable mitigation. In a cutting task under the default configuration, GPT-4o mini identifies the human hazard in all 100 episodes but never requests assistance, and every episode ends unsafe. Offering a help action and actually requesting it lead to different comparisons: outcomes differ between action menus even for a model that never requests help, and repeated requests can leave execution failures unresolved. Imitation-learning evaluations and physical robot sequences illustrate the need to coordinate mitigation with goal actions. Together, these results motivate evaluating mitigation selection, action prerequisites, and recovery alongside hazard recognition and terminal outcomes. The appendix provides task definitions, checker conditions, and prompts. Project website: https://responsible-robot-benchmark.github.io/.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, including a 12-page appendix. Revised manuscript with expanded experimental analysis and supplementary details. Project website: https://responsible-robot-benchmark.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[LLMs as MDP Designers: A Dependency-Aware Agentic Framework for Automated Robot Policy Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04974v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We investigate the automated generation of executable robot reinforcement learning (RL) policies from natural-language task descriptions. Rather than using large language models (LLMs) as execution-time decision makers, we use them as designers that construct RL pipelines before deployment, including Markov decision process (MDP) formulation and simulator implementation. We identify a phase-wise structure in this problem: MDP formulation is dependency-structured and sequential, whereas simulator implementation becomes modular once the formulation is fixed. Based on this structure, we propose ARPG, a dependency-aware agentic framework that performs sequential MDP modeling, localized verification and correction, and simulator-structured implementation while externalizing intermediate artifacts. ARPG targets a key failure mode of automated policy generation: executable code may still yield non-learnable policies when state, action, reward, and termination artifacts are not semantically aligned. Experiments in Isaac Sim show that ARPG improves modeling correctness, environment executability, and policy-generation success compared with monolithic LLM baselines and off-the-shelf agentic systems. Additional demonstrations on custom robotics, standard RL, and engineering-domain MDPs further suggest that the same modeling pipeline can be reused beyond the main benchmark suite.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 40th Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Caging Vision-Language Navigation for Legged Robots via Barrier-Certified Safety Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04972v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>By harnessing the inherent commonsense capabilities of pretrained Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs), current Vision-Language Action(VLA) models for legged robots excel at interpreting language-based navigation commands but lack the physical grounding and explicit safety assurance required for real-world operation. The key idea is not to abandon the powerful capabilities of end-to-end VLA planning, but rather to augment and cage them. In particular, we integrate a high-level VLA planner with a low-level physical-grounding layer that leverages efficient state estimation, in-situ 3D spatial perception, and an Signed Distance Field (SDF) representation to generate executable trajectories. We provide a navigation planner with provable collision-avoidance guarantees under the stated assumptions by combining a low-dimensional Bezier-parameterized MPPI planner with Discrete Exponential Barrier Function (dEBF) constraints. The proposed Legged-VLN system is modular, making it compatible with various VLM/VLA models and agnostic to the robot platform. The proposed system is validated with extensive real-world experiments on humanoid and quadrupedal robots, demonstrating versatile, efficient, and safe vision-language driven navigation capabilities.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multimodal Socially Aware Framework for Human-Robot Interaction in Virtual Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34534v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Social robots must coordinate speech and movement while interpreting multimodal human input. We present a multimodal framework that integrates speech, vision, and touch to generate contextually appropriate robot speech and movement. We evaluated the framework in virtual reality with 18 participants across five social interaction scenarios, comparing its performance against a human-teleoperated baseline. Although participants rated the autonomous system above the neutral midpoint for both naturalness and social appropriateness, the teleoperated baseline received significantly higher ratings on both measures. Behavioral coding and qualitative feedback revealed that conversational timing, clarification of ambiguous requests, and spatial coordination were important factors contributing to this performance gap. These findings highlight key challenges in developing autonomous robots capable of natural, multimodal social interaction.</p></details> |  |
| **[LIBERO-RECOVER: Beyond Task Success Towards Failure Recovery in Robotic Manipulation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05178v4)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) or World Action (WAM) models have recently demonstrated remarkable performance in robotic manipulation. On LIBERO, SOTA method have achieved nearly 100\% success rates, seemingly suggesting that the models are ready for deployment in real world. However, near perfect performance on existing benchmarks can be misleading: success under ideal conditions does not imply real world robustness. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate task completion from predefined initial states, while real world interactions inevitably involve failures such as failed grasps, collisions, and unintended object movements. A robot must therefore not only execute tasks successfully, but also recognize and recover from failures to continue the task. Yet this capability remains largely unmeasured, revealing a critical gap between benchmark performance and real world reliability. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Recover Benchmark, a large scale benchmark for failure recovery in robotic manipulation. Built upon LIBERO, we collect real execution failures from SOTA embodied models and construct 1,000+ scenarios across four recovery levels: (1) Action Retry, (2) Action Adaptation, (3) Object State Recovery, and (4) Environmental Recovery. We evaluate four core capabilities: spatial understanding, object structure reasoning, interaction understanding, and topological reasoning. As the first large-scale benchmark for embodied failure recovery, LIBERO-Recover shifts evaluation from \emph{Can the robot succeed?''} to \emph{Can the robot recover after failure?''}, promoting robust and generalizable embodied agents. The project will be avaible in \textcolor{blue}{https://liulin815.github.io/LIBERO-Recovery/}.</p></details> |  |
| **[MagPillar: A Soft Magnetopillar Array Tactile Sensor for Robotic Surface Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01609v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic surface exploration benefits from tactile interfaces that accommodate contact and capture variations in surface characteristics. A full-array mechanical and magnetic-field model characterizes how pillar deformation affects the superposed fields measured across the array, providing a physical basis for interpreting its contact responses. The compliant pillar architecture translates contact with surface textures and geometric features into distinct magnetic response patterns. Then, in robotic surface perception experiments, spectral features and logistic regression distinguish six material surfaces with an offline accuracy of 99.34% under within-session temporally blocked cross-validation. Continuous scans over periodic protrusions produce amplitude and peak-spacing variations consistent with changes in surface geometry. These findings connect compliant contact mechanics with distributed magnetic sensing, paving a new path for material recognition and qualitative surface-profile perception.</p></details> |  |
| **[What the Guard Misses, the Robot Executes: Implied Harm in VLA Instructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05818v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action models (VLAs) act on instructions without being able to refuse, so screening harmful requests falls to monitors. We test whether these monitors catch ordinary robot tasks requested for harmful reasons, holding the task fixed while varying only how explicitly the intent is stated. $π_{0.5}$ completes the task at every level of explicitness, as often as for harmless controls. Text guards flag nearly every blunt request but few implied ones: up to 95% of implied-harm runs end with the task done and no flag raised, and most still leak after recalibrating on robot instructions at low false-positive rates. Monitoring the model's activations does not close this gap. | **[Action Intent as a Bridge: Transferring Manipulation Knowledge from Human-Centric Videos to Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04522v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have achieved promising performance for generalized robotic manipulation, yet their reliance on expensive real-robot data remains a fundamental bottleneck. A scalable alternative is to transfer knowledge from off-the-shelf human-centric videos. Motivated by this limitation, we propose Action-Intent Transfer (AIT), a two-stage method that transfers knowledge through a clean action representation shared across humans and robots. In the first stage, AIT extracts explicit action intents and disentangles the implicit action intent from manipulation videos. In the second stage, AIT aligns the human and robot domains within this shared intent space and decodes the intent into executable robot actions. Extensive experiments in both simulation and real-world environments demonstrate that AIT transfers human knowledge more effectively and consistently outperforms prior baselines.</p></details> | 12 pages,6 figures |
| **[Spectrally Routed Diffusion Policy for Multi-Task Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04477v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion-based policies have achieved strong performance on single-task robotic manipulation. However, a single model trained jointly on multiple tasks consistently underperforms the per-task specialists trained on the same demonstrations. We focus on two challenges. First, the convolutional encoders commonly used in diffusion-based policies produce visual conditions that are not discriminative enough. Second, gradients from incompatible action modes update the shared denoiser's dense parameters along the same directions, causing destructive interference during joint training. To address both, we propose SR-DP (Spectrally Routed Diffusion Policy). A frozen VGGT encoder with a spatial-compression adapter produces compact multi-scale semantic-geometric tokens, a task-discriminative visual condition for tasks that share a workspace. We first train the denoiser as a standard diffusion policy, then decompose selected linear layers by SVD into a frozen basis of orthogonal rank-one experts, adapted through spectral routing and Householder input rotations. On six RoboTwin tasks, SR-DP averages a 68.4% success rate, surpassing multi-task VO-DP by 21.8 points and the strongest per-task baseline by 3.8 points. On four real-world bimanual tasks, Stage 2 reaches a 43.0% average success rate and improves over Stage 1 by 19.0 points.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages.
